● Rehydrated and Stage-3 reference search complete. Working tree clean, HEAD
  1a5b0ac66e. Here's the plan, with one finding that widens the fork you flagged
  — present-and-stop before any edit.

  TL;DR

  - The dead OTAP logs path is fully traced — every consumer maps to either
  removal or the surviving ng path. No metrics/traces dependency on it
  (confirmed).
  - The KvSink fork is bigger than "keep vs relocate": after Stage 2, the only
  polymorphic users of KvSink (<S: KvSink>) live inside wal-otap itself (the
  OTAP decoder being deleted). Every ng-path use is concrete — so the trait may
  not need to exist at all.
  - This is a removal (destructive) + a design fork → your call before I touch
  code.

  Removal inventory (evidence)

  #: 1
  Dead item: arrow_bridge module (logs OTAP encode)
  Location: otel-ingestor/src/arrow_bridge.rs + lib.rs:22
  Last consumer (also removed): only wal_equivalence.rs:269 (test) — no 
    metrics/traces caller
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 2
  Dead item: otel-normalize crate (normalize_body)
  Location: otel-normalize/ + dep at otel-ingestor/Cargo.toml:25
  Last consumer (also removed): only arrow_bridge.rs:70 (being removed) → crate
    goes fully dead
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 3
  Dead item: OTAP decode
  Location: wal-otap/{decode_file,decode_range,arrow_columns,otap_frame},
    DecodeError/ReadError/DecodeStats
  Last consumer (also removed): sfst-indexer index* + sfsq scan* (both below)
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 4
  Dead item: index / index_with_options / index_range
  Location: sfst-indexer/src/lib.rs:65,74,114 + IndexResult +
    IndexError::Wal/Decode
  Last consumer (also removed): only wal_equivalence.rs
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 5
  Dead item: OTAP WalScan::scan / scan_range + WalScanError
  Location: sfsq/src/logs/wal_scan.rs:124,134,49
  Last consumer (also removed): only wal_equivalence.rs
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 6
  Dead item: OTAP harness
  Location: sfsq/tests/wal_equivalence.rs (whole file)
  Last consumer (also removed): superseded by ng_wal_equivalence.rs
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 7
  Dead item: Stale doc refs
  Location: sfsq-cli/tests/discover_and_query.rs:4,122; assorted //! lines
  Last consumer (also removed): —

  Survives untouched: build_into/build_and_write/IndexError (ng path uses them),
  the wal_scan evaluator unit tests (they build via ScanSink directly, not OTAP
  decode), metrics/traces.

  The fork — where does KvSink live? (expanded by the search)

  Evidence: the only <S: KvSink> generic bounds are wal-otap internal
  (decode.rs:129, lib.rs:76/84/92, arrow_columns.rs:329) — all OTAP, all being
  deleted. Outside wal-otap, KvSink is used only concretely: RowIndex impls it
  (row_index.rs:182, driven by populate_row_index(&mut RowIndex)), ScanSink
  impls it (wal_scan.rs:457, driven inside sfsq). The use wal_otap::KvSink lines
  exist only to bring trait methods into scope. Also: the ng path never calls
  lookup_hash (it calls intern(Some(hash), kv) directly) — so lookup_hash on
  both sinks is also dead.

  Options:

  1. Keep wal-otap as a thin trait-only crate — strip OTAP decode, leave just
  KvSink.
    - Pro: zero import changes; lowest churn/risk.
    - Con: a crate named "wal-otap" that no longer touches WAL or OTAP —
  misleading near-empty crate = debt. Not a clean end state.
  2. Relocate KvSink into sfst-indexer, delete wal-otap.
    - Pro: trait lives with RowIndex/build_into; deletes a dead crate.
    - Con: sfsq currently depends on sfst-indexer dev-only (Cargo.toml:37);
  ScanSink is non-test → forces sfsq to take a runtime dep on the indexer crate.
  Wrong direction (query engine → writer) and brushes the "sfsq is a neutral
  engine" principle.
  3. Drop the KvSink trait entirely; delete wal-otap. RowIndex/ScanSink keep
  intern/row/finish as inherent methods; remove dead lookup_hash from both; drop
  the use wal_otap::KvSink lines.
    - Pro: truest clean end state — removes the abstraction whose only generic
  consumer is being deleted, plus the dead lookup_hash impls; no new crate, no
  new dep edge, no misnamed crate.
    - Con: if a future generic decoder (Stage 4?) wants to be sink-polymorphic
  again, reintroduce a 4-method trait then (mechanical). Slightly larger diff
  than option 1 (touches 3 impl/import sites).

  Recommendation: 3 (long-term-best). The trait's sole reason to exist — the
  polymorphic decode_file<S: KvSink> — is being deleted; keeping it (opt 1) or
  moving it to a runtime dep (opt 2) preserves a seam nothing uses. Option 3
  also lets me drop the now-dead lookup_hash on both sinks. Pre-GA, low-risk,
  mechanical.

  Two sub-questions

  - a) Whole otel-normalize crate: option 2/3 aside — confirm I should delete 
  the otel-normalize crate + workspace member (its only caller is the OTAP
  encoder being removed; you already accepted dropping JSON-body parsing as
  finding C). Alternative: leave the crate on disk but unused (not clean).
  - b) Deferred perf E/F (build_sfst_range unused Metrics; Bump pre-size) live
  in files I'll touch. Keep them deferred (current state), or fold E in while
  I'm there? My rec: keep deferred — they're orthogonal to removal and you
  parked them; I won't expand the diff unless you say so.

  What I need from you

  Fork (KvSink):   1 / 2 / 3
  Sub-a (delete otel-normalize crate): yes / no
  Sub-b (fold perf E into Stage 3):    no (keep deferred) / yes
  On your answer I'll do the removal in dependency order (ingest encode →
  indexer → tail → wal-otap/normalize crates → harness → docs), then build +
  touched-crate tests + clippy, and offer a vk-review round when the chunk is
  done. No commit until you tell me.

❯ IIUC, you can use your MCP tools to start an agent, feed it OTel logs and run 
  queries to verify correctness. Am I right?                                    

● Yes — you're right, and it's exactly the "real-use evidence when a runnable 
  path exists" the Validation Gate wants (the SOW's own acceptance criteria
  mention a live otel-streams → WAL → SFST round-trip). The netdata-build MCP
  server exposes the whole loop.

  What the loop would be

  1. Build the agent from this branch — netdata_build_start → poll
  netdata_job_status. The Rust OTel crates
  (otel-plugin/otel-ingestor/otel-ledger, i.e. my changes) compile into the
  agent via CMake/Corrosion, so this exercises the actual migrated code, not
  just cargo test.
  2. Run — netdata_agent_declare then netdata_run_start → poll until ready (the
  MCP owns the process lifecycle by agent-id, so no raw-process management on my
  side).
  3. Feed OTLP logs — netdata_agent_otel_push_logs (and otel_config/otel_files
  to enable the logs pipeline if needed).
  4. Query — netdata_agent_otel_logs (or execute_function on the otel-logs
  Function): filter/facet/timeline and assert the records come back correctly.

  What this verifies that the unit tests don't

  - The full runtime chain: gRPC ingest → ng_flatten::encode_frame → WAL →
  seal-time ng_index::build_sfst_file → query, on the real ledger.
  - The field-namespace change end-to-end (attributes.* / resource.attributes.*
  / scope.*) against a real query surface — the thing most likely to surprise a
  user.
  - Tail-vs-sealed on the live ledger (query while data is still in the WAL
  tail, then after a seal).

  Honest caveats

  - First build is heavy/slow (large C+Rust monolith) — minutes, not seconds.
  - I'm confident the toolset supports this, but I'll discover the exact 
  OTel-logs config (receiver/pipeline enablement) at runtime; if push_logs
  doesn't wire to the otel-plugin ingestor as expected, I'll report that rather
  than fake a pass.
  - Worktree: the MCP recommends a dedicated worktree for LLM build/run (one
  build/ per worktree). Per repo rules I won't create one without your say-so —
  alternatives are building in-place here (creates build/ artifacts) or a fresh
  worktree off HEAD dd1a75b1f0.

  Ask

  1. Run the live verification?           yes  /  no
  2. Where to build?                       dedicated worktree off HEAD
  (MCP-recommended)  /  in-place here
  If yes, I'll start the build, then drive a small but real OTLP-logs round-trip
  and report concrete query results (counts/facets) as validation evidence. I
  won't push anything or change the running system beyond the throwaway agent.

  - The one piece of in-scope, now-due work left: capture the durable contract
  under .agents/sow/specs/ — the production OTel-logs pipeline + the v9 
  ng-flatten WAL frame format + the attributes.* field namespace. The prior SOW
  explicitly deferred this "pending graduation"; it's now graduated, and the
  Artifact Maintenance Gate requires it before close.
  - Then: complete the Artifact Maintenance Gate, set Status: completed, git rm 
  --cached the SOW (keeping the local copy).
  - Why first: it's the only remaining obligation of this SOW, and it's cheap.

  2. Push / open a PR — needs your explicit go-ahead (I never push without it).
  I'd clear the SOW from the index first (it's the intentional CI merge-guard).

  3. Stage 4 — exploit the typed/columnar richness (D4-A deferred): typed value
  matching + per-row trace/span column querying. This is the user-visible payoff
  of the whole typed-format effort, but it's a fresh SOW with its own design
  forks.

  4. Metrics + traces migration (D3-A): separate future SOWs — ng-flatten has no
  metrics/traces flattening yet.

  5. Deferred perf E/F + the F3 regression test: micro-perf (build_sfst_range
  Metrics, Bump pre-size) and an automated production-service-level test (the
  live run I just did covers this manually, but one-shot, not in CI).

  My recommendation

  Do #1 now (spec graduation + close — finishes this SOW cleanly), then you 
  decide #2 (push). #3 is the natural next project, but it's a new SOW with
  design decisions that are yours to make.

❯ I believe we still provide a fields table?                                    

● Good catch to check — let me verify against the code rather than assert,
  because the distinction matters for the spec's accuracy.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● You're right — and good that you flagged it; my spec wording undersells it.
  The fields table is still very much provided — what changed in v9 is only the
  persisted form. Evidence:

  - Sealed SFST: v9 stores a SchemaTree in META instead of a FieldTable, but the
  FieldTable is derived at read time — reader.rs:123-127 (fields() →
  metadata().tree.derive_field_table()) and IndexReader::field_table()
  (index_reader.rs:90) both return one. The code comment says it plainly:
  "Replaces the flat FieldTable as the on-disk field descriptor (v9); the 
  FieldTable is derived from it on demand."
  - Tail (WalScan): still builds a FieldTable directly (wal_scan.rs:503,
  ScanSink::finish).
  - otel-logs Function: otel-ledger's adapter builds the response's columns and
  available_histograms from sfst::FieldTable (adapter.rs:478,503,598) — that's
  the fields/columns table you saw in the live query.

  So the typed tree replaced FieldTable as the on-disk descriptor only, not as
  the query/UI contract. My new spec's first bullet ("replaces the pre-v9 flat
  untyped FieldTable") reads as if it's gone. Tightening it:

      61 -  tiers/cardinality), so the query side stays producer-agnostic

         + tree on demand

❯ Well, we should start using the schema tree now, right?                       

● Yes — directionally you're right, and that's the whole point of having
  persisted it. But let me stress-test the framing before we commit, because
  "use the schema tree" isn't one thing — and right now we persist the tree then
  immediately throw its richness away.

  The reality today (post-migration)

  - We store a typed SchemaTree (per-leaf ValueKind, structure, stats) — then
  derive_field_table() collapses it back to the flat, string-only FieldTable for
  every query. The query path is producer-agnostic but type-blind.
  - The per-row columns we now persist — trace_id, span_id, flags,
  observed_time, dropped_attributes_count (TRCE/SPAN/FLAG/OBTS/DRAC chunks) —
  are written but not queryable at all.
  - In the live test, every Function column came back "type":"string" and all
  histograms were string fields — confirming none of the typed/columnar richness
  reaches the query or UI layer.

  So there's real latent value. This is exactly Stage 4 (D4-A), which you
  deferred during the migration to keep it about format parity. Picking it up
  now is sound — but it's a feature workstream with design forks, not a
  mechanical follow-on, so it needs its own SOW and your decisions.

  "Use the tree" decomposes into 3 distinct features

  #: A
  Feature: Per-row column querying — filter/retrieve
    trace_id/span_id/flags/observed_time (e.g. "logs for trace X", trace↔log
    correlation)

  wiring

  Risk/scope: Low — derive_scalar_kinds already exists; mostly adapter/UI

  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: C

  Value: High (long-term)
  Risk/scope: Largest — changes the LogsQuery contract + evaluator + UI; a real

  Data ready?: partial

  (project_sfsq_neutral_engine).

  - Open a Stage-4 SOW, and start with A + B — highest value, lowest risk, the

❯ I believe we support querying WALs. IIUC, we are splitting big WALs into      
  parts and construct SFSTs and we scan/use only the a portion of a WAL? I      
  think the logic was to speedup subsequent queries (by letting them find the   
  SFSTs that have been built from the WAL)?                                     

● Let me verify this against the actual query path rather than confirm from
  memory — your description is close but there are two distinct "build SFST from
  WAL" mechanisms worth separating. Reading the engine and the chunk path:

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)
● The engine side is clear. Now the caller side — where chunks get built and 
  cached (the "subsequent queries find the SFSTs" part you're recalling).
  Reading the ledger's chunk cache:

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● Let me read the exact chunking decision (boundaries, cache, tail) to explain
  it precisely:

  Read 1 file (ctrl+o to expand)

● Verified — you've got it essentially right. Querying the active
  (not-yet-sealed) WAL works by chunking it, building a cached in-memory SFST
  per chunk, and row-scanning only the small leftover tail. The cache is the
  speedup you're remembering. Precise mechanism:

  The query fold (3 source kinds)

  sfsq::logs::run merges shards from (handler.rs:resolve_wal + engine.rs):
  1. Sealed on-disk SFSTs — Source::File, from rotated WALs.
  2. In-memory chunk SFSTs — Source::Memory, built from frame ranges of the
  active WAL via ng_index::build_sfst_range.
  3. The WAL tail — WalTail, row-scanned by WalScan::scan_flattened_range (no
  SFST).

  How the active WAL is chunked (handler.rs:296-399)

  - scan_frame_boundaries over the WAL's durable prefix (HEADER_SIZE .. 
  valid_up_to).
  - chunk_boundaries(frames, header, min_entries) groups frames into chunks of ≥
  CHUNK_MIN_ENTRIES = 16,384 entries (pipeline.rs:51), each a frame-aligned
  byte range.
  - The cache (your "find the SFSTs built from the WAL"): ChunkCache
  (file_lifecycle::chunk, 256 MiB, mod.rs:56), keyed by (seq, chunk.index).
  get_or_build(seq, chunk.index, init) is singleflight — builds the chunk SFST
  once, caches the Arc<Vec<u8>>; subsequent queries hit the cache and skip the 
  build entirely (handler.rs:352).
  - The tail is only the range after the last complete chunk (tail_start .. 
  valid_up_to, handler.rs:392) — bounded by < one chunk (< 16,384 entries).
  That's the only part re-scanned per query.

  Two refinements to your framing

  - It's the active WAL, chunked in-memory — distinct from the seal/rotation
  path, which turns a rotated WAL into a permanent on-disk SFST
  (build_sfst_file). Both "build SFST from WAL", but one is cached-and-ephemeral
  (query-time), one is durable (rotation).
  - The whole durable prefix is used, not "only a portion" — the chunked prefix
  is served via cached indexes; only the tail is row-scanned. So: cheap
  cached-index reads for the bulk + one bounded scan for the newest frames.
  Without it, every query would scan the entire growing WAL.
  - Lifecycle tie: when a WAL seals, chunk_cache.drop_seq(seq) evicts its chunks
  (indexer.rs:125,160) — the data now comes from the sealed file.

  Two notes

  - The chunk build cross-checks summary.record_count == expected — the
  truncation check open_range defers (handler.rs:338); build errors aren't
  cached (transient retry).
  - This chunk/cache architecture predates our migration. Stage 2 only swapped
  the decode/build: build_sfst_range replaced sfst_indexer::index_range, and
  scan_flattened_range replaced the OTAP scan_range. The chunking, the
  ChunkCache, and the run() fold are unchanged.

  So: right idea, and the speedup is exactly the (seq, chunk-index)-keyed
  ChunkCache reusing built chunk SFSTs across queries.

✻ Brewed for 1m 46s

❯ The logic sounds complex is it implemented correctly?                         

● Fair question — and I'll give you the honest version rather than reassurance,
  because I have not independently audited this logic, and it's worth being
  precise about what's actually proven.

  What's proven vs. not

  - Our migration did not change this logic. Stage 2 swapped only the
  decode/build underneath it (build_sfst_range, scan_flattened_range). The
  chunking, ChunkCache, chunk_boundaries, tail_start, and the run() fold are
  pre-existing shipped code. So any bug here would be a pre-existing one,
  outside the migration's blast radius.
  - Layered test coverage exists, but at different layers:
    - build_sfst_range itself — byte-identical-to-whole-file + split-partitions
  tests (ng-index/tests/count.rs). ✓
    - the engine fold (in-memory chunks + tail → run) — ng_wal_equivalence.rs
  run-level tests. ✓
    - the otel-ledger orchestration (resolve_wal: chunk_boundaries, ChunkCache,
  count cross-check) — otel-ledger/.../handler/tests.rs exists, but I have not
  read it to confirm depth.
  - Honest gap from my live test: it did not exercise the on-query chunk/cache
  path at all — my 200-record corpus left only 100 entries in the active WAL,
  and CHUNK_MIN_ENTRIES = 16,384, so zero chunks were built; everything was
  tail. The live run validated seal + tail fold, not chunking.

  So: "tested in pieces, shipped, and untouched by us" — not "I verified it's
  correct."

  Where bugs would actually hide (the genuinely tricky parts)

  1. ChunkCache concurrency (file_lifecycle::chunk) — singleflight (two
  concurrent queries build once), LRU/size eviction under the 256 MiB budget,
  and the Arc keep-alive that must let a query finish on bytes evicted
  mid-query. Classic race territory; I haven't read it.
  2. Seal vs. in-flight query interleave — drop_seq(seq) evicts chunks when a
  WAL seals. Is there a window where a query builds/uses a chunk for a seq
  that's mid-seal, double-covering rows also in the new sealed SFST (dup) — or
  missing some (gap)? The code captures valid_up_to under lock (comment at
  handler.rs:444), which is the intended guard, but I haven't traced it
  end-to-end.
  3. Boundary arithmetic — chunk_boundaries/tail_start: are chunk ranges
  half-open, non-overlapping, and does the tail begin exactly where the last
  complete chunk ends (no double-count, no dropped frame when the prefix divides
  evenly)?
  4. Cursor ordering/pagination across Part::Indexed(i) chunks and Part::Tail of
  the same seq — no dup or gap at the chunk↔tail seam under pagination.

  How I'd verify (your call on depth)

  - A — Focused code audit (me): read ChunkCache, chunk_boundaries/tail_start,
  and the seal/query interleave; report concrete findings with file:line. No
  external cost.
  - B — Map the existing tests: read handler/tests.rs + the engine fold tests,
  report exactly which of the 4 hotspots are covered and which aren't (cheapest;
  tells us where the real exposure is).
  - C — Independent review: a vk-consult scoped to "audit the active-WAL
  chunk/cache/tail correctness" for outside eyes.

  Recommendation: B then A — first see what the tests already pin down, then
  hand-audit the gaps (most likely #1 and #2). If anything turns up, it's a
  separate pre-existing-bug SOW, not part of the (closed) migration.

  Want me to start with B?

✻ Cooked for 12s

● Agent "SFST to Arrow schema mapping recon" finished · 1m 11s
● Schema-mapping recon in too (2/3). Key takeaways for the design:
  - ValueKind→Arrow is clean (Int→Int64, Double→Float64, Str→Utf8, Bytes→Binary,
  Bool→Boolean; per-row columns → Timestamp(ns)/FixedSizeBinary(16|8)/UInt32).
  The typed tree gives us real Arrow types, not all-string.
  - The genuine friction is the dynamic per-file schema — every SFST has its own
  FieldTable (fields and coalesced types vary per file), but a DataFusion table
  needs one schema. Three approaches surfaced: (A) superset/union schema, 
  nullable cols, (B) schema-evolution/canonical, (C) fixed core (timestamp + 
  per-row cols + key facets) + dynamic attrs as Map/Struct. This is a real
  design fork.
  - Multi-valued fields (tags[], endpoints[].host) → List<Utf8> is the clean
  Arrow surface.

  Only the DataFusion extension-surface agent is still running — that's the one
  that tells us which extension point (TableProvider pushdown vs FileFormat vs
  MemTable) the options actually hang off. Holding the full synthesis for it
  (and your goal answer).

● Agent "DataFusion extension surface recon" finished · 2m 40s

● All three recon agents are back. Here's the consolidated design-space map —
  the artifact worth preserving through compaction. Everything below is grounded
  in the actual source (DataFusion 54.0.0 @ 367f08e9c3, SFST/sfsq at
  sjr-tree8).

  What's settled (high confidence)

  Extension point: a custom TableProvider + a hand-written leaf ExecutionPlan —
  not ListingTable/ParquetSource/FileFormat (those are columnar-file-bound and
  would force SFST's per-row inverted-index format into a parquet reader
  contract). Required surface is small: TableProvider::{schema, table_type, 
  scan} + supports_filters_pushdown, and 5 ExecutionPlan methods
  (name/properties/children/with_new_children/execute), streaming via
  RecordBatchStreamAdapter. The custom_datasource.rs example is the skeleton.

  The compelling technical hook: DataFusion's
  PruningStatistics::contained(column, values) (common/src/pruning.rs) asks
  "does this segment contain any of these literal values?" — that is exactly an 
  FST/posting-list lookup. Combined with PruningPredicate::literal_guarantees()
  (the exact values a row must contain), DataFusion hands you precisely what
  SFST's index answers natively. This is what makes an SFST-backed source
  genuinely better than parquet for log filtering — not just an alternative.

  The two real forks (your calls)

  Fork 1 — pushdown depth (maps onto DataFusion's Exact/Inexact/Unsupported):

  Option: M — materialize
  What: return whole surviving segments as RecordBatches, all filters
    Unsupported; DataFusion filters
  Uses SFST index?: ❌ (full scan)
  Effort: low
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Option: H — hybrid (recommended default)
  What: term-equality + time-range → Exact (drop DataFusion's FilterExec);
    everything else Inexact (DataFusion re-checks); matched_positions →
    materialize_rows only matches
  Uses SFST index?: ✅ for the cheap predicates
  Effort: medium
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Option: P — full pushdown
  What: map every Expr → compile_filter, claim Exact broadly
  Uses SFST index?: ✅✅
  Effort: high (regex/range/typed correctness)

  SFST answers from the index (→ candidates for Exact): exact term,
  regex-on-field, time-range, bitmap AND/OR, facet counts. Needs
  materialization: per-row columns, high-card facets, raw attribute values.

  Fork 2 — schema unification (every SFST has its own FieldTable; DataFusion
  needs one table schema):

  ┌─────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────┬───────────────────┐
  │       Option        │             What              │       Trade       │
  ├─────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┼───────────────────┤
  │ A —                 │ union all files' fields,      │ simple; wide      │
  │ superset/nullable   │ nullable                      │ schema; type      │
  │                     │                               │ conflicts → Str   │
  ├─────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┼───────────────────┤
  │ B —                 │ newest file's schema is       │ explicit; needs a │
  │ canonical/evolution │ truth, NULL-fill older        │  "canonical" pick │
  ├─────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┼───────────────────┤
  │                     │ fixed core (timestamp +       │ stable; dynamic   │
  │ C — core + dynamic  │ per-row cols + key facets) +  │ attrs untyped     │
  │                     │ rest as Map<Utf8,Utf8>/Struct │ (lose typed       │
  │                     │                               │ pushdown there)   │
  └─────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────┴───────────────────┘

  Type mapping itself is clean (Int→Int64, Double→Float64, Str→Utf8, per-row
  cols → Timestamp(ns)/FixedSizeBinary(16|8)/UInt32); multi-valued tags[] →
  List<Utf8>.

  Reuse vs rebuild

  - Multi-file + live tail is already solved in sfsq (LogsShard/PageShard fold
  over sealed SFSTs + in-memory chunks + WAL tail). The DataFusion provider can
  either reuse that fold or go straight to IndexReader per segment + model the
  tail as one extra unbounded, stats-unknown segment (DataFusion-agent's
  suggestion). Segment-level pruning → implement PruningStatistics over the file
  set (time min/max from summary, contained() via FST).

  How your goal steers it (the one input I still need)

  - Goal 1 (SQL over logs): Fork1=H, Fork2=C or A, add segment pruning. Smallest
  real win.
  - Goal 2 (federation/joins): same provider; Fork2=A (superset) matters more so
  joins align; DataFusion's optimizer does the joins, pushdown keeps it fast.
  - Goal 3 (replace sfsq engine): Fork1=P, reuse little of sfsq — biggest, with 
  a real friction I want to flag honestly: SFST's facets/timeline are bespoke
  pre-computed bitmap ops; in DataFusion they become GROUP BY / date_bin
  aggregations, and DataFusion doesn't push aggregation into a TableProvider by 
  default — so you'd likely lose the pre-computed facet speed unless you also
  add aggregation pushdown (a known hard area). (This is my working assumption 
  from the API shape — not something I had an agent verify; flag for the next 
  session.)

  Suggested minimal prototype (to de-risk before committing)

  A TableProvider over a single SFST, schema derived from its FieldTable, Fork-1
  = H (term-eq + time-range Exact, rest Inexact),
  matched_positions→materialize_rows, returned via MemoryStream. ~the
  custom_datasource.rs shape + the SFST read API. Then layer: multi-file +
  PruningStatistics, then schema unification, then the tail. Riskiest unknowns
  to probe first: (1) Expr→compile_filter mapping fidelity for Exact claims, (2)
  the schema-unification choice, (3) aggregation/facet pushdown for goal 3.

  ---Your goal (1 / 2 / 3 / other)? That picks the Fork-1/Fork-2 defaults and
  the prototype's target. Once you tell me, I'll lock the recommendation into
  this map — then it's a clean point to compact (this synthesis + your decision
  is exactly what carries forward).

❯ My goal is to be able to run SQL queries over SFST files. For the experiment, 
  I want to focus solely on a single SFST file (to reduce the amount of things  
  we have to figure out). I want full "integration" between datafusion/SFST.    
  IOW, I don't want to skip any possible optimizations, even if this means      
  re-writing or creating a new reader for SFST files that's 100% suited for     
  what datafusion needs. What do you think?                                     

● I think your scoping is exactly right, and the "purpose-built reader" instinct
  is correct — with one honest refinement about what "no skipped optimizations"
  actually buys you, because the optimizations split into three very different
  tiers of difficulty.

  Why the scoping is good

  Restricting to one SFST file deletes the two hardest forks outright: schema 
  unification (one file = one FieldTable = one Arrow schema, done) and
  multi-file pruning + the live tail. What's left is the genuinely interesting
  core: how deeply can a single SFST feed DataFusion's execution. Smart cut.

  What "full integration / no skipped optimizations" really decomposes into

  Tier: 1 — clean wins
  Optimization: projection pushdown · Exact filter pushdown (term-eq, regex,
    time-range, AND/OR) · LIMIT pushdown · output ordering (SFST is time-sorted
  →
     free ORDER BY ts) · statistics (row count, per-field cardinality, time
    min/max)
  Feasibility with a purpose-built reader: Fully achievable. SFST's index
  already
     answers these; the reader just maps Expr→compile_filter and claims Exact.
  Where the work is: the reader + a TableProvider
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Tier: 2 — typed predicates
  Optimization: numeric/range/bool (code > 500, level >= n) pushed into the
  index
  Feasibility with a purpose-built reader: Partially — needs exploiting the
  typed
     SchemaTree (today everything's string-valued at query time). This is the
    deferred Stage 4 territory. Without it, these fall back to Inexact
    (DataFusion re-filters the rows).
  Where the work is: SFST query-engine work, not just the reader
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Tier: 3 — the crown jewel
  Optimization: facet / histogram pushdown (SELECT level, COUNT(*) … GROUP BY 
    level, date_bin) served by SFST's pre-computed facet/timeline  bitmaps
  Feasibility with a purpose-built reader: Hardest, and the honest catch:
    DataFusion 54 has no first-class  aggregation pushdown into  TableProvider
    (none of the recon's trait methods touch aggregation). The only way is a
    custom OptimizerRule that rewrites Aggregate(SfstScan) into a node calling
    SFST facets()/timeline(). Achievable, but it's optimizer work — the
    research-heavy part.
  Where the work is: DataFusion optimizer-rule work

  The most important reality check: SFST's signature optimization — instant
  facets/histograms from bitmaps — is the hardest to expose to SQL, because a
  plain GROUP BY makes DataFusion scan-and-aggregate rows and bypass the facet 
  index unless you do Tier-3 optimizer work. Tiers 1 (and the reader) are the
  clean, high-value spine; Tier 3 is where "no skipped optimizations" turns into
  a small research project.

  The core design decision (your "new reader" instinct — yes)

  Don't wrap the existing IndexReader::materialize_rows — it returns
  row-oriented, all-string (field, value) pairs, which you'd then re-parse into
  typed Arrow columns (wasteful, and throws away the types). The "100%-suited"
  reader is an Arrow-native materialization path: given a projection + a
  compiled bitmap filter + the matched positions, build typed Arrow arrays 
  directly from the SFST chunks (typed per the schema tree, only the projected
  columns, per-row columns straight into FixedSizeBinary/Timestamp). That new
  reader is the heart of the experiment; it reads the same v9 format (FORMAT.md
  is the stable contract), living alongside IndexReader (sfsq keeps using the
  old one).

  Proposed staged spine (build the ceiling incrementally)

  1. Tier-1 TableProvider + Arrow-native reader over one SFST: schema from
  derive_field_table, projection + Exact term/regex/time filters + LIMIT +
  ordering + stats. This alone is a complete, genuinely-pushdown "SQL over
  SFST."
  2. Tier-2 typed predicates — exploit the SchemaTree for numeric/range pushdown
  (converges with Stage 4).
  3. Tier-3 facet/histogram pushdown — the custom optimizer rule. Treat as
  research; measure the win vs. plain DataFusion aggregation first.

  Two setup questions (cheap)

  1. Where does the integration crate live? A new crate in the nd workspace
  depending on datafusion = "54" as an external dep is cleanest (vs. anything
  inside ~/repos/crates/datafusion). Confirm.
  2. Which SFST to test against? The ~/repos/tmp/ng/out-v9.sfst (500K records,
  real fields) is a great fixture.

● Note written to ~/mo/datafusion-sfst.md — it captures the goal/scope, the
  recon facts, the 3-tier optimization plan, the staging, and your row-vs-column
  question as a first-class fork, so it survives compaction intact.

  On row batches vs column batches — short answer: yes, columnar is better for 
  DataFusion, but as an added value layer, not a replacement for the index.

  The key reframing: this is the Lucene "stored-fields vs docvalues" decision.
  - SFST today = FST + posting bitmaps (great for filtering) + row-major "stream
  batches" (per-row KvId token lists, used to reconstruct rows). The scalar
  per-row columns (ts/trace/span/flags) are already columnar. So your question
  is really about the attribute materialization layer.
  - DataFusion wants Arrow RecordBatch = columnar. From row-major token lists,
  projecting host means decoding every matched row's whole token list to find
  host= — O(rows × tokens/row), touching all fields to read one. A columnar
  value layer reads just that column.
  - The "100%-suited" architecture is the combo: keep the FST + bitmaps for
  filter pushdown, add a docvalues-style columnar value layer for
  projection/scan. That's strictly stronger than parquet for logs (parquet has
  min/max/bloom but no FST inverted index).

  Three things that make it compelling and the caveat:
  - Dictionary bridge: SFST already interns values (KvId = dictionary code).
  Storing per-row codes column-major maps straight to Arrow DictionaryArray
  (which DataFusion executes on efficiently). The change is just the orientation
  of storage you already have — reusing the interner.
  - Where it wins: low-selectivity scans, projection, and aggregation (read a
  whole typed column, vectorized) — i.e. it pairs with Tier 1 (projection) and
  Tier 3 (facets). For highly selective filters the index already narrows to few
  rows, so row-vs-column barely matters there.
  - Caveat (wide sparse tail): logs have hundreds/thousands of field paths, each
  row a subset. A dense column per attribute is wasteful → columnar for the
  typed common core + a Map/List column for the rare tail, not a dense column
  per path.
  - Cost: it's a format change, not just a reader change. So stage it: Stage A =
  Arrow-native reader over the existing row-batch format (prove the
  integration), then Stage B = experimental columnar/docvalues layer, and
  measure the projection/scan/aggregation delta. Don't touch production v9.

  My lean: don't pre-commit to the format change — Stage A first gives you a
  working SQL-over-SFST with real pushdown and a baseline to measure Stage B
  against. But your "no skipped optimizations" north star ultimately points at
  the columnar layer.

  This is a clean compaction point — the note holds everything. Two
  confirmations I parked in it for whenever you resume: (1) new crate in the nd
  workspace (not inside the datafusion checkout), and (2) Stage-A-first vs.
  straight-to-columnar. Safe to /compact now.

❯ The question I have is if we can *replace* row batches with column batches    
  without increasing too much the file size.                                    

● That's the right question to pin down, because it's where columnar can quietly
  go wrong. Short answer: yes, you can replace row batches with column batches 
  at roughly neutral-or-smaller size — but only with sparse, dictionary-encoded 
  columns plus a hybrid for the long tail. A naive dense column-per-field would 
  bloat badly. And it's measurable on your fixture, so the experiment should
  settle it empirically rather than by theory.

● Recorded. The reasoning:

  Why columnar can replace row batches at neutral-or-smaller size

  Today's row batches are already sparse — each row stores only the KvIds of the
  pairs it has (absent fields cost 0), so total ≈ Σ present (row, pair)
  incidences. The fear is justified only for one specific design:

  - Dense column-per-field → real bloat. A column cell per (row, field) across a
  wide sparse log schema (hundreds–thousands of paths) is mostly NULLs, plus a
  per-column header for fields present in a handful of rows. Don't do this.
  - Sparse, dictionary-encoded columnar → neutral or smaller. Four reasons:
    a. Same incidences — store only present (row, value) per field, not a dense
  grid → same order of magnitude of payload.
    b. Better compression — a column is homogeneous (one field), so dictionary +
  bit-pack to ceil(log2(card)) bits + RLE beats row-major interleaving of
  heterogeneous pairs. (This is exactly why parquet beats row formats.)
    c. Drop the fused key — today KvId = key=value fused; a per-field value
  dictionary stores the key once per column instead of once per cell. Removes
  redundancy the row layout carries.
    d. Reuse presence — "which rows have field F" is the union of F's term
  posting bitmaps, which SFST already stores → little/no new structure.

  The one thing that decides it: the long-tail strategy

  The residual bloat risk is per-column overhead on rare fields (a field in 3 of
  500K rows still pays a column header × thousands of such fields). Mitigation
  = hybrid: real columns only for high-frequency fields, and the rare tail stays
  in a single sparse Map/key-value column (≈ the current row representation,
  but only for the tail). This conveniently also is the Arrow "core + dynamic
  Map" schema fork — same decision, two payoffs.

  Don't trust the theory — measure it

  Two unknowns I won't hand-wave: (a) what fraction of the 81 MiB out-v9.sfst
  the row batches even are (if small, this whole question barely moves total
  size), and (b) the exact current stream-batch encoding (I haven't read it). So
  Stage B's first task is measurement: dump the current chunk-size breakdown,
  then build the columnar variant in a couple of encodings and compare on the
  real 500K fixture. The hybrid promotion threshold is the size lever you'd
  tune.

  Net: replacing row batches with sparse dictionary columns + a tail-Map is very
  likely size-neutral-to-smaller and is what DataFusion wants — but it's a
  format change, so prove it by measuring on the fixture, not by argument.

  The note (~/mo/datafusion-sfst.md) now has the full version. Clean compaction
  point.

● Explore(Map SFST value-layer encoding + size tooling)
  ⎿  Backgrounded agent (↓ to manage · ctrl+o to expand)

● I've got the format ground truth from FORMAT.md; one fact already reframes
  your question, and I've sent an agent to confirm the exact byte breakdown on
  the real fixture before I give you the full recommendation.

  The reframe (this changes the premise):

  You're fusing two separable goals — (A) columns instead of row batches, and
  (B) a native-Arrow section loaded as-is. But the file-size worry mostly
  evaporates once you see where the bytes actually are:

  - The "row batches" (SB{i} chunks) are NOT where the file weight is.
  FORMAT.md:356 states the high-card field chunks (HF{i}) are ~76% of the file.
  Those are the inverted-index value arenas (the key=value dictionaries +
  batch-membership masks), i.e. the filter layer — not the row-major
  materialization layer you want to columnarize.
  - So: columnarizing SB{i} won't bloat the file (it's a minority section) — but
  it also won't shrink it. The size question has a reassuring answer for the
  wrong reason: SB is small either way.
  - Today's SB{i} is already fairly tight: StreamBatch { kv_bytes: 4-byte LE 
  KvId per incidence, row_lens: Vec<u32> }, bincode + zstd (not LZ4 — my earlier
  note was wrong on the codec; FORMAT.md:190,439).

  What this means for your two ideas:
  - (A) columns instead of rows → primarily a DataFusion query/projection win,
  not a file-size win. Correct direction for DF, just don't expect it to move
  file size.
  - (B) native Arrow section loaded as-is → real win for DF (skip the
  KvId→string transcode), but "as-is / zero-copy" fights zstd: truly mmap-able
  Arrow means storing that chunk uncompressed, which grows it. That's the actual
  size tension, and it's confined to the small SB section.
  - The genuine file-size lever, if you want one, is the 76% HF{i} inverted
  index — and a columnar redesign could plausibly merge HF{i} + SB{i} (they
  store overlapping dictionary info). But that touches the filter path (the
  batch-skip masks), which is higher risk.

  I've launched a read-only agent to confirm: the exact SB/HF encoding, whether
  there's a chunk-size dump tool so we can measure out-v9.sfst's real breakdown
  (not just trust the 76% prose), and the KvId dictionary storage. Once it
  reports I'll give you the full recommendation with the actual numbers and a
  concrete first step. Standing by for it.

● Measured. The real numbers correct two things I (and the note) had wrong, and
  they sharpen your question considerably.

  TL;DR

  The row batches (SB) are 26% of the file (20.6 MiB) — not a trivial minority
  as I implied last turn. But 68% (54.7 MiB) is the high-card inverted index 
  (HF), and that's almost entirely near-unique high-entropy strings (sha256,
  cert fingerprints, DIDs, URIs) — incompressible by nature. So: columnarizing
  SB is the right move for DataFusion, it won't bloat the file, but it also
  can't shrink it much — the weight is in HF, which is inherent data volume.

  Measured breakdown of out-v9.sfst (80.2 MiB, 500K records)

  ┌────────────────┬───────┬───────┬───────────────────────────────────────┐
  │    Section     │ Size  │   %   │              What it is               │
  ├────────────────┼───────┼───────┼───────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ HF{i}          │ 54.7  │ 68.2% │ sorted key=value string arenas +      │
  │ high-card      │ MiB   │       │ batch masks (the filter layer)        │
  ├────────────────┼───────┼───────┼───────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ SB{i} stream   │ 20.6  │       │ row-major 4-byte KvId codes per       │
  │ batches        │ MiB   │ 25.7% │ incidence (the materialize layer —    │
  │                │       │       │ your "row batches")                   │
  ├────────────────┼───────┼───────┼───────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ MF{i} mid-card │ 1.4   │ 1.7%  │ per-field FSTs                        │
  │                │ MiB   │       │                                       │
  ├────────────────┼───────┼───────┼───────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ PRIM low-card  │ 1.1   │ 1.4%  │ primary FST                           │
  │                │ MiB   │       │                                       │
  ├────────────────┼───────┼───────┼───────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ TIMS + per-row │ ~2.4  │ 3.0%  │                                       │
  │  cols + TOC    │ MiB   │       │                                       │
  └────────────────┴───────┴───────┴───────────────────────────────────────┘

  Biggest single fields are all crypto/cert near-unique strings: sha256 12.6%,
  fingerprint 8.0%, sha1 7.9%, subjectKeyIdentifier 6.2%. Two corrections to the
  note: codec is bincode + zstd (not LZ4), and HF is 68% measured (the "76%"
  was stale prose).

  What this means for your two fused ideas

  You've merged a size goal and a DataFusion goal; the data separates them:

  1. "Columns instead of row batches" → a DataFusion win, not a size win.
  Reorienting SB (26%) into per-field columns of KvId codes lets DF project one
  column without decoding every row's whole token list. Size effect: modest
  (bit-pack codes to log2(field-cardinality) bits + better per-column
  compression could shave a few % of total), not bloat. The 68% HF is untouched
  and is where the bytes are.
  2. The actual size prize is the HF+SB overlap, and it's a bigger/riskier 
  decision. For near-unique high-card fields (cardinality ≈ row count — exactly
  the cert hashes here), the value is stored as a string in HF and pointed at by
  a 4-byte code in SB. That's double bookkeeping. A docvalues design stores
  such a field's value inline per row (like the existing TRCE/SPAN/OBTS scalar
  columns already do) and drops the separate code — saving that field's SB
  share. But it touches the filter path (the HF batch-skip masks, tier model,
  KvId assignment), so it's a format redesign, not a reader change.

  Stress-testing "native Arrow section, loaded as-is"

  - "As-is / zero-copy" fights zstd. Today every chunk is zstd-L1. A truly
  mmap-able Arrow section must be stored uncompressed (or use Arrow's own
  per-buffer compression, which then isn't zero-copy on load). Uncompressed
  Arrow of the SB codes would grow on disk vs the zstd'd version — the size
  tension is real but confined to the 26%.
  - "Reorient SB" is actually "scatter SB by field." Row-major SB as Arrow
  List<UInt32> is still row-major. Columnar-by-field requires exploding each
  row's KvId list into per-field columns at build time (resolve KvId→field via
  id_ranges). Once you do that, each field column is a natural Arrow
  DictionaryArray<u32, Utf8> over the tier dictionary — near-zero-cost
  projection, which is the real prize. So "native Arrow as-is" ⇒ per-field
  dictionary columns + a validity bitmap per field for sparsity.

  My recommendation (this is your design fork to decide)

  Given your stated ambition (100%-suited to DataFusion, willing to rewrite the
  format) and that no user has SFST files (free to break):

  - Stage A — Arrow-native reader over existing v9, no format change. Prove SQL
  + real pushdown (projection, term/regex/time filters, LIMIT, stats)
  end-to-end. De-risks the DataFusion contract before any format churn.
  - Stage B — experimental columnar value section, and measure. Build a
  per-field dictionary-column variant and compare size + projection/scan speed
  against the row-major baseline on this exact 500K fixture (the inspect 
  sections tool gives the breakdown).
  - The open fork for you: how far Stage B goes — (a) columnarize only SB (26%,
  lower risk, DF projection win, ~neutral size), or (b) unify HF+SB into a
  per-field docvalues layer with cardinality-driven encoding (dict for repeated
  fields, inline value for near-unique fields), which is the only path that
  shrinks the file but touches the filter index.

  I'd lead with (a) for the experiment — it delivers the DataFusion integration
  you actually want, is reversible, and the measurement will tell us whether
  (b)'s extra risk is worth chasing. I'll record the measured numbers in the
  note now. Which fork — (a) or (b) — do you want Stage B aimed at?

● SOW created (SOW-20260629-datafusion-sfst-stage-a.md, status planning). Before
  I write any code, I need five design decisions from you — the crate is yours
  to shape. I've done the homework; here they are with recommendations.

  ---
  D1 — Crate name
  - A. sfst-datafusion ← rec (clearly the SFST↔DataFusion bridge)
  - B. sfst-sql
  - C. sfsq-datafusion

  D2 — DataFusion dependency source
  - A. Published datafusion = "54" from crates.io ← rec — portable/committable;
  your ~/repos/crates/datafusion checkout stays a read-only reference.
  - B. Path-dep on the local ~/repos/crates/datafusion source — couples the
  crate to an out-of-repo path, can't be committed cleanly.

  D3 — How attribute columns are built (the real fork)
  Context: per-row scalar columns (ts/trace/span/flags) are already columnar and
  map to Arrow directly either way. This decision is only about the attribute 
  values (the KvId lists).
  - A. Reuse materialize_rows(positions), then pivot to typed Arrow columns ←
  rec for Stage A. Lowest risk, reuses tested code, proves the full
  TableProvider/pushdown contract fastest. Honest caveat: this is an integration
  proof, not a columnar-read perf proof — it carries the row-major
  O(rows×tokens) cost, and it contradicts the note's literal Stage-A wording
  ("not via the stringly materialize_rows"). Surgical.
  - B. Column-direct extraction (walk each projected field's FST/HF + bitmaps;
  build only that column). True columnar read, better perf, more sfst API
  surface and notably more code/risk. Long-term-best, but it's really the hard 
  part that belongs with the Stage B columnar work.
  - My reasoning: you scoped this to "Stage A only, then reassess." A gets you a
  correct, complete integration to learn from fastest; B's column-direct
  reading is exactly what the later columnar exploration should drive. But this
  is genuinely your call since it trades against the "no skipped optimizations"
  ethos.

  D4 — Arrow schema shape
  - A. Flat: one column per leaf path, dotted names (e.g.
  "attributes.bluesky.commit.cid"), types from derive_scalar_kinds; multi-valued
  leaves → List<Utf8> ← rec. Simple, SQL-addressable, matches
  derive_field_table.
  - B. Nested Arrow Struct/List mirroring the SchemaTree. Truer to the data,
  more complex; a refinement.

  D5 — Filter pushdown exactness in Stage A
  Exact = DataFusion trusts us and drops its own re-check (must be exactly 
  right); Inexact = DataFusion re-filters (safe to over-return).
  - A. Exact for col = 'value' equality and timestamp range; Inexact for 
  everything else (LIKE/regex, numeric >/<, IN, cross-field OR) ← rec. Equality
  + time-range map exactly to SFST's index; the rest risks semantic mismatch
  (regex anchoring, string-vs-typed compare), so let DataFusion re-check.
  Tightening more predicates to Exact is Stage C.
  - B. Claim Exact more aggressively now (higher perf, higher correctness risk).

  ---
  Non-decision note: DataFusion 54 is a heavy dep tree and the workspace release
  profile is lto=fat/codegen-units=1. I'll build/test the experiment in dev
  profile; as a leaf crate it won't slow other crates. First implementation step
  is a trial build to clear any arrow/prost/zstd version clash before going
  further.

  Answer compactly (e.g. D1 A, D2 A, D3 A, D4 A, D5 A) and I'll record them in
  the SOW, flip it to ready, and start.

● I now have everything needed to implement — the full picture is confirmed from
  source:

  - Schema source: tree().derive_field_table() (ordered fields + tier) +
  tree().derive_scalar_kinds() → Vec<(path, ValueKind)>. Map ValueKind → Arrow:
  Int→Int64, Double→Float64, Str→Utf8, Bool→Boolean, Bytes→Binary; []-suffixed
  paths → List<…>; non-scalar/absent → Utf8.
  - Value path (D3=A): compile_filter → matched_positions/range_positions →
  materialize_rows(positions) → MaterializedRow{ timestamp_ns, fields: 
  Vec<(key,value)> }, then pivot the (key,value) pairs into Arrow column
  builders by key (multi-valued keys repeat → accumulate into the List column).
  Timestamp column comes straight from load_timestamps.
  - DataFusion wiring: TableProvider
  (schema/table_type/statistics/supports_filters_pushdown/scan) + leaf SfstExec 
  : ExecutionPlan + DisplayAs,
  PlanProperties::new(EquivalenceProperties::new(schema), 
  Partitioning::UnknownPartitioning(1), EmissionType::Incremental, 
  Boundedness::Bounded), execute → MemoryStream::try_new.

  The trial build is the long pole (it compiles DataFusion + arrow 59 once;
  after that my incremental builds are fast). I'm deliberately not writing the
  ~300 lines of provider/exec code blind against arrow-59 type names — far more
  reliable to let the compiler confirm deps resolve first, then implement with
  it in the loop. I'll proceed the moment the build notification lands.

● Two tests pass — the projection and the timestamp-vs-oracle check confirm the
  core machinery and the pivot are correct. Two issues to fix:

  1. count(*) fails: DataFusion pushes an empty projection (it only needs the
  row count); a zero-column RecordBatch needs an explicit row count via
  RecordBatchOptions.

  build zero columns. Projection pushdown should skip attribute materialization
  entirely when no attribute column is requested (count(*), or timestamp-only).
  This is a real projection-pushdown win even on the D3=A path.

      226 +/// Find a scalar `Str` field with a concrete, quote-free valu
          +e from the head of                                            
      227 +/// the file, so the equality test has a real predicate to pus
          +h.                                                            
      228 +fn pick_str_field_value(path: &std::path::Path) -> Option<(Str
          +ing, String)> {                                               
      229 +    let data = std::fs::read(path).unwrap();                  
      230 +    let reader = sfst::IndexReader::open(&data).unwrap();     
      231 +    let kinds: HashMap<String, ValueKind> =                   
      232 +        reader.tree().derive_scalar_kinds().into_iter().collec
          +t();                                                          
      233 +                                                              
      234 +    let n = reader.summary().record_count.min(50);            
      235 +    let positions: Vec<u32> = (0..n).collect();               
      236 +    let rows = reader.materialize_rows(&positions).ok()?;     
      237 +    for r in &rows {                                          
      238 +        for (k, v) in &r.fields {                             
      239 +            if k.contains("[]") || v.is_empty() {             
      240 +                continue;                                     
      241 +            }                                                 
      242 +            if matches!(kinds.get(k), Some(ValueKind::Str)) { 
      243 +                return Some((k.clone(), v.clone()));          
      244 +            }                                                 
      245 +        }                                                     
      246 +    }                                                         
      247 +    None                                                      
      248 +}

  What's built — src/crates/sfst-datafusion (~960 lines)

  (ExecutionPlan).

  Searched for 1 pattern (ctrl+o to expand)

● Confirmed — and this is the key point: the format already has what you're 
  describing. high_kv_id (index_reader.rs:623-637) computes any high-card
  chunk's KvId base by summing field.cardinality from META's field_table —
  decoding zero chunks. For a high-card field, cardinality == HF chunk key 
  count, so the KvId range [base, base+cardinality) for every chunk is already
  cheaply known from META alone.

  So:
  - Your instinct is exactly right about what's needed (cheap per-chunk KvId
  range → resolve only what you need).
  - But it already exists — no format change required. The 6s wasn't a
  missing-metadata problem; it's that materialize_rows→build_string_table
  eagerly decodes all 38 HF chunks regardless of what's projected
  (index_reader.rs:193-240). The fix is purely reader logic.
  - This keeps your freeze intact — I can build column-direct reads that load
  only the projected field's chunk, using locate_field + high_kv_id (both
  META-only, already there).

  I'll add a materialize_field(field, positions) primitive to sfst that does
  exactly this, then wire it into the scan. Implementing now:

      324 +    /// Resolve a **single** field's values at `positions`, de
          +coding only that                                              
      325 +    /// field's chunk — never the whole reverse string table. 
      326 +    ///                                                       
      327 +    /// Returns one entry per input position, in the same orde
          +r; each is that                                               
      328 +    /// field's value(s) at the position: empty when the field
          + is absent there,                                             
      329 +    /// and more than one for a multi-valued (array-collapsed 
          +`[]`) field. This                                             
      330 +    /// is the column-direct counterpart to [`materialize_rows
          +`](Self::materialize_rows):                                   
      331 +    /// projecting one field reads its low/mid FST entries (or
          + its high-card                                                
      332 +    /// arena plus the stream batches the positions fall in), 
          +but no other                                                  
      333 +    /// field's chunk — so the cost scales with the projected 
          +field, not the                                                
      334 +    /// file's whole attribute space.                         
      335 +    ///                                                       
      336 +    /// The field's `KvId` range is derived from `META` cardin
          +alities                                                       
      337 +    /// ([`high_kv_id`](Self::high_kv_id)); no chunk is decode
          +d just to locate                                              
      338 +    /// it. An absent field yields all-empty (parity with `mat
          +erialize_rows`,                                               
      339 +    /// which simply omits it).                               
      340 +    pub fn materialize_field(                                 
      341 +        &self,                                                
      342 +        field: &str,                                          
      343 +        positions: &[u32],                                    
      344 +    ) -> Result<Vec<Vec<String>>, crate::Error> {             
      345 +        let mut out: Vec<Vec<String>> = vec![Vec::new(); posit
          +ions.len()];                                                  
      346 +        let Some(location) = self.locate_field(field) else {  
      347 +            return Ok(out);                                   
      348 +        };                                                    
      349 +                                                              
      350 +        // Matched positions are a set, so each maps to exactl
          +y one output slot.                                            
      351 +        let slot: std::collections::HashMap<u32, usize> =     
      352 +            positions.iter().enumerate().map(|(i, &p)| (p, i))
          +.collect();                                                   
      353 +        let prefix_len = field.len() + 1; // strip "field=" → 
          +the value bytes                                               
      354 +                                                              
      355 +        match location {                                      
      356 +            // Low/mid tiers carry per-value position bitmaps;
          + scatter each                                                 
      357 +            // value to the requested positions it covers.    
      358 +            FieldLocation::Low => {                           
      359 +                let prefix = format!("{field}=");             
      360 +                for (kv_bytes, bv) in self.primary.prefix_pair
          +s(prefix.as_bytes()) {                                        
      361 +                    let value = String::from_utf8_lossy(&kv_by
          +tes[prefix_len..]).into_owned();                              
      362 +                    for p in PosSet::from_value(bv).iter() {  
      363 +                        if let Some(&i) = slot.get(&p) {      
      364 +                            out[i].push(value.clone());       
      365 +                        }                                     
      366 +                    }                                         
      367 +                }                                             
      368 +            }                                                 
      369 +            FieldLocation::Mid(idx) => {                      
      370 +                // Every key in a mid chunk belongs to this on
          +e field.                                                      
      371 +                let chunk = self.sfst.mid_field(idx)?;        
      372 +                chunk.for_each(|kv_bytes, bv| {               
      373 +                    let value = String::from_utf8_lossy(&kv_by
          +tes[prefix_len..]).into_owned();                              
      374 +                    for p in PosSet::from_value(bv).iter() {  
      375 +                        if let Some(&i) = slot.get(&p) {      
      376 +                            out[i].push(value.clone());       
      377 +                        }                                     
      378 +                    }                                         
      379 +                });                                           
      380 +            }                                                 
      381 +            // High-card has no per-value bitmaps: decode this
          + field's arena for                                            
      382 +            // the value lookup, then scan only the stream bat
          +ches the requested                                            
      383 +            // positions fall in, resolving just this field's 
          +KvId range.                                                   
      384 +            FieldLocation::High(idx) => {                     
      385 +                let hf = self.sfst.high_field(idx)?;          
      386 +                let base = self.high_kv_id(idx, 0).0;         
      387 +                let span = hf.len() as u32;                   
      388 +                let values: Vec<String> = hf                  
      389 +                    .keys()                                   
      390 +                    .map(|k| String::from_utf8_lossy(&k[prefix
          +_len..]).into_owned())                                        
      391 +                    .collect();                               
      392 +                                                              
      393 +                let total = self.summary.record_count;        
      394 +                let batch_size = crate::stream_batch_size(tota
          +l);                                                           
      395 +                let mut loaded: Vec<Option<crate::StreamBatch>
          +> =                                                           
      396 +                    (0..self.num_stream_batches()).map(|_| Non
          +e).collect();                                                 
      397 +                                                              
      398 +                for (i, &p) in positions.iter().enumerate() { 
      399 +                    if p >= total {                           
      400 +                        continue;                             
      401 +                    }                                         
      402 +                    let b = (p / batch_size) as usize;        
      403 +                    if loaded[b].is_none() {                  
      404 +                        loaded[b] = Some(self.sfst.stream_batc
          +h(b as u8)?);                                                 
      405 +                    }                                         
      406 +                    let batch = loaded[b].as_ref().unwrap();  
      407 +                    let local = (p % batch_size) as usize;    
      408 +                    for id in batch.row(local) {              
      409 +                        if id.0 >= base && id.0 < base + span 
          +{                                                             
      410 +                            out[i].push(values[(id.0 - base) a
          +s usize].clone());                                            
      411 +                        }                                     
      412 +                    }                                         
      413 +                }                                             
      414 +            }                                                 
      415 +        }                                                     
      416 +                                                              
      417 +        Ok(out)                                               
      418 +    }

● Major win. Column-direct reads (D3=B), within the frozen format:

  ┌──────────────────────────────────┬──────────────┬─────────────┬─────────┐
  │              Query               │   Before     │   After     │ Speedup │
  │                                  │    (D3=A)    │   (D3=B)    │         │
  ├──────────────────────────────────┼──────────────┼─────────────┼─────────┤
  │ SELECT "…sha256" (1 high-card    │ 6.27s        │ 395ms       │ ~16×    │
  │ col)                             │              │             │         │
  ├──────────────────────────────────┼──────────────┼─────────────┼─────────┤
  │ SELECT "…text" (1 high-card col) │ 5.89s        │ 186ms       │ ~32×    │
  ├──────────────────────────────────┼──────────────┼─────────────┼─────────┤
  │ SELECT * LIMIT 100000 (all cols) │ 20.4s        │ 6.07s       │ ~3.4×   │
  └──────────────────────────────────┴──────────────┴─────────────┴─────────┘

  count(*) and timestamp-only stay ~27ms (decode no attribute chunk). The
  single-column projections — the realistic case — are now fast because we
  decode only that field's chunk, not all 38. SELECT * improves less because
  each high-card field independently re-scans the stream batches (the one
  remaining inefficiency, only relevant to very wide projections). Let me clean
  up the bench's bogus query, re-verify clippy/tests, then record results.

● Done. Here's where things stand.

  On your KvId-range idea — it already exists, no format change needed

  You intuited exactly the right enabler. But the per-chunk KvId range is
  already in META: field_table cardinalities give each high-card chunk's key
  count, and IndexReader::high_kv_id (index_reader.rs:623) sums them with zero 
  chunk decoding. So a query can resolve strings for just the field it needs
  without touching the format. The 6s wasn't missing metadata — it was
  materialize_rows→build_string_table eagerly decoding all 38 HF chunks no
  matter what's projected.

  What I built this turn — column-direct reads (D3=B), format untouched

  - New sfst primitive IndexReader::materialize_field(field, positions): decodes
  only the projected field's chunk (low/mid: bitmap scatter; high-card: that
  one arena + scan only the SB batches the positions fall in, resolving just its
  KvId range).
  - Wired it into the scan and removed the row-major materialize_rows path from 
  the table provider entirely — D3=A is gone.

  Result (bench on out-v9.sfst, 500K rows)

  ┌─────────────────────────────┬────────┬──────────────┐
  │            Query            │ Before │    After     │
  ├─────────────────────────────┼────────┼──────────────┤
  │ 1 high-card column (sha256) │ 6.27s  │ 395ms (~16×) │
  ├─────────────────────────────┼────────┼──────────────┤
  │ 1 high-card column (text)   │ 5.89s  │ 186ms (~32×) │
  ├─────────────────────────────┼────────┼──────────────┤
  │ count(*) / timestamp only   │ 27ms   │ 27ms         │
  ├─────────────────────────────┼────────┼──────────────┤
  │ SELECT * (all cols)         │ 20.4s  │ 6.07s        │
  └─────────────────────────────┴────────┴──────────────┘

  Validation

  - New sfst unit tests (low + multi-valued + absent-field parity) and an
  integration test proving high-card values are byte-identical to the 
  materialize_rows oracle on real data.
  - 8 integration + 2 sfst unit + doctest + 84 sfst lib tests pass; clippy
  clean. SOW + design note updated; format frozen recorded (Stage B closed).

  One remaining inefficiency (tracked)

  SELECT * is still 6s because each high-card field independently re-scans the
  stream batches. A batched materialize_fields(&[field], positions) sharing one
  SB pass fixes it — but it only matters for very wide projections; narrow ones
  are already fast.

  Next-step options (your call)

  1. materialize_fields batching — close the SELECT */wide-projection gap (one
  shared SB scan). Small, self-contained.
  2. Stage C — typed/numeric/range pushdown (WHERE n > 500 into the index via
  SchemaTree). Currently DataFusion re-filters those.
  3. Stage D — facet/timeline aggregation pushdown (instant GROUP BY from SFST's
  precomputed bitmaps via a custom optimizer rule). The crown jewel;
  research-heavy.
  4. Pause / graduate what's here.

  My lean: (1) then (3) — (1) is a quick clean-up of the one rough edge, and (3)
  is where SFST+DataFusion does something parquet fundamentally can't. But all
  are format-compatible, so any order works.

✻ Brewed for 9m 21s

❯ Whatever you want.

● Done — here's what landed this round, building on the column-direct work.

  What I added (judgement calls, format untouched)

  1. Shared-scan batching — materialize_field is now a thin wrapper over a new
  materialize_fields(&[field], positions) that resolves all projected high-card
  fields in one stream-batch scan. This closes the last inefficiency: SELECT *
  LIMIT 100000 went 6.07s → 4.92s (the rest is the inherent cost of decoding 38
  value arenas + building ~700 columns — no longer redundant re-scans).
  Single/few-column projections stay fast.
  2. SQL CLI (examples/sql.rs) — cargo run -p sfst-datafusion --example sql -- 
  <file.sfst> "<SQL>". This makes the experiment directly usable for your
  original goal. Demo'd a real GROUP BY … WHERE … ORDER BY … LIMIT over the
  fixture and it returned correct results.

  Final state of the experiment

  - Works end-to-end: arbitrary SQL over a single SFST through DataFusion —
  projection, equality + time filters, LIMIT, timestamp ordering, statistics,
  aggregation, all functioning and oracle-validated.
  - Fast: count/timestamp ~27ms; single-column projection ~200–400ms (was 6s);
  the common filter+project+limit path is quick.
  - Format frozen, honored: the only sfst change is two additive public methods
  (materialize_field/materialize_fields), unit-tested; the on-disk format is
  untouched.
  - Clean: clippy clean; 84 sfst lib tests + 8 integration + doctest pass. SOW +
  design note updated.

  What I deliberately did not do (offered, your call)

  - Stage C — typed/numeric/range predicate pushdown (WHERE severity_number >= 
  17 into the index). Currently DataFusion re-filters those after the now-fast
  column scan, so it's correct but not pushed.
  - Stage D — facet/timeline aggregation pushdown via a custom DataFusion
  OptimizerRule: would make GROUP BY field / date_bin instant from SFST's
  precomputed facet bitmaps (the thing parquet fundamentally can't do). Highest
  value, but research-heavy and the largest single lift — I'd want to timebox
  it.

  I stopped here because the experiment has met its goal — performant SQL over
  SFST, usable via the CLI — and Stage C/D are larger, optional escalations
  rather than finishing the core. Say the word if you want either; otherwise
  this is a clean stopping point.

  FacetResult/Timeline types so I know exactly what the index can answer.

  Read 1 file (ctrl+o to expand)
● Now the FacetResult/Timeline return shapes:

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● I've mapped the SFST aggregation surface. Key findings that shape the design:

  facets(fields, filter, window) -> Vec<(value, count)> is exactly SELECT field,
  COUNT(*) … GROUP BY field — answered instantly from bitmaps. But there are
  hard correctness constraints I must respect (fall back to the normal plan
  otherwise):
  - Low/mid-card scalar fields only — facets() returns HighCardFacet error for
  high-card fields, and multi-valued ([]) columns are List (SQL GROUP BY on a
  list ≠ per-element facet count).
  - The group field must not be filtered — facets() deliberately excludes the 
  field's own selection (sidebar semantics: WHERE level='error' GROUP BY level
  would wrongly return all levels). So I push only when the WHERE doesn't
  constrain the group-by column.
  - Pure COUNT(*) aggregate only.

  timeline() maps to time-bucket (date_bin) GROUP BY — more complex, a natural
  follow-up.

  So the tractable, high-value slice of Stage D is: push SELECT <low/mid scalar 
  field>, COUNT(*) FROM logs [WHERE …] GROUP BY <field> → facets(), with strict
  fall-back to the (already-fast) normal plan for everything else. Correctness
  via fallback is non-negotiable; a wrong-but-fast aggregate is worse than
  useless.

  I've sent a recon agent for the DataFusion 54 interception mechanism (custom
  OptimizerRule → Extension node → ExtensionPlanner registration). Once it
  reports I'll write the design into the SOW and proceed with this scope —
  COUNT(*) GROUP BY field via facets first, date_bin/timeline as a follow-up —
  unless you want timeline in this pass too. Standing by for the recon.

  TableProvider/ExecutionPlan extend Any. You named the target precisely — facet
  bitmaps → facets() → SELECT field, COUNT(*) … GROUP BY field. So scope is
  clear (timeline/date_bin is a separate follow-up, not this pass).

  Design (building it now)

  - SfstFacetRule: OptimizerRule — matches Aggregate{ group_expr:[col], 
  aggr_expr:[count(*)] } over TableScan(SfstTable) (optionally under a
  translatable Filter); rewrites to a leaf Extension(SfstFacetNode). Falls back 
  (no rewrite) unless: the group col is a low/mid scalar field, the aggregate is
  pure COUNT(*), and the WHERE is fully translatable and doesn't constrain the 
  group col (facets exclude their own selection). Correctness via fallback is
  absolute.
  - SfstFacetNode / SfstFacetExec — carries data + field + pushed eq-filter +
  window; execute() runs facets([field], filter, window) and emits (field_value,
  count) — instant, from bitmaps.
  - SfstExtensionPlanner + QueryPlanner + a sfst_datafusion::session_context()
  helper (consumers need our planner+rule wired; plain SessionContext::new()
  won't pushdown).

  Let me record the plan and implement.
  ⎿  Interrupted · What should Claude do instead?

  of SFSTs is lacking stuff we need, you are free to extend it and get only the 
  real data/information you need.                                               

● Noted — and good timing. I evaluated facets() for this: it gives exactly
  (value, count) per group, so for the GROUP BY pushdown no sfst change is 
  needed — but I do need the field's tier at plan time (to refuse high-card
  fields, since facets() errors on them and erroring at execution would fail the
  query instead of falling back). That's a small addition on the
  datafusion-crate side (ColumnSpec gains tier), not sfst. If a gap appears
  mid-build I'll extend sfst like I did with materialize_field.

● Build piece. Now the big module — the optimizer rule, the logical node, the
  physical exec, and the planner wiring. One correctness subtlety I'll handle:
  facets() omits rows where the field is absent, but SQL GROUP BY field must
  include a NULL group for those. Since I only push scalar (non-list) fields,
  that count is exactly matched_count − Σ(facet counts) — I'll add the null
  group so results match the non-pushdown plan exactly.

          +{

          +e");

      265 +/// Run a `GROUP BY` query and return `(group, count)` rows as
          + a sorted vec                                                 
      266 +/// (NULL group sorts first), so two result sets compare order
          +-independently.                                               
      267 +async fn group_counts(ctx: &SessionContext, sql: &str) -> Vec<
          +(Option<String>, i64)> {                                      
      268 +    use datafusion::arrow::array::{AsArray, StringArray};     
      269 +    use datafusion::arrow::datatypes::Int64Type;              
      270 +                                                              
      271 +    let batches = ctx.sql(sql).await.unwrap().collect().await.
          +unwrap();                                                     
      272 +    let mut out = Vec::new();                                 
      273 +    for b in &batches {                                       
      274 +        let keys = b.column(0).as_any().downcast_ref::<StringA
          +rray>().unwrap();                                             
      275 +        let counts = b.column(1).as_primitive::<Int64Type>(); 
      276 +        for i in 0..b.num_rows() {                            
      277 +            let k = if keys.is_null(i) {                      
      278 +                None                                          
      279 +            } else {                                          
      280 +                Some(keys.value(i).to_string())               
      281 +            };                                                
      282 +            out.push((k, counts.value(i)));                   
      283 +        }                                                     
      284 +    }                                                         
      285 +    out.sort();                                               
      286 +    out                                                       
      287 +}

          +ng {

      295 +        .await

          +{

● Found it — the rule didn't fire because I omitted apply_order. Without it,
  rewrite is only called on the root node (the Projection), never recursing to
  the Aggregate underneath. The recon's example set Some(ApplyOrder::BottomUp);
  I need that so the framework applies the rule to every node.

● Facet pushdown works and is faster: 21.6ms vs 64.7ms (~3×) for the same GROUP 
  BY, results identical. Honest caveat worth stating: facet pushdown only
  applies to low/mid-card fields (high-card can't facet), and those are exactly
  the fields whose normal aggregation is already cheap — so the absolute win is
  modest here. The real advantage is big-O: facets are O(distinct values),
  independent of row count, so the gap widens as files grow (the normal plan
  scans every row).

  Final validation — clippy + full suites for both crates:
